### Popup command

`feat/popup-command` (branched from `master`, fork only): `terminal.popup {argv, cwd?, hold?, wait?, width?, height?}` starts a program on this computer as `hc run --bg --no-parent` does (`rm` unless `wait`) and opens the `terminal.peek` overlay for it in the same step, so there is no retry while the window learns about the terminal. `herdr-app popup [--cwd DIR] [--hold] [--wait] [--size WxH] [--] cmd args…` drives it over the control socket; with `--wait` it sends `{"wait": terminal}`, which the window answers from the core's events once the terminal finishes, then removes the terminal, and the CLI (command-line interface) exits with its exit code, 128 + the signal for a program a signal killed (the core's `signal`), or 1 (removed before it ended, the window is not open or the popup failed). A popup lives only while it can be seen: closing the peek overlay by any path (✕, Esc's `overlay.close`, the backdrop, a mod's `closeOverlay`, the escape-hatch reset) ends the program the last `terminal.popup` started (`end_popped`, from `close_overlay`), and so does a new popup (`replace_popped`). `popped` tracks it, with the core's `created_ms`, until it is ended or a listing shows it finished, gone or another terminal at its address, since addresses come back after a core restart; a wait popup's terminal stays until its waiter has the exit code. A `terminal.peek` of an existing terminal is never ended, and a plain `terminal.peek`, such as a link clicked in a popup, only hides the popup under it (`open_peek` removes the overlay without closing it), which ends when that peek closes. ↗ keep (`terminal.keep`) makes the popup the person's tab: the app, not the peek bar, closes the peek once the keep is under way, as any close does, so a popup hidden under a link's peek ends (a keep turned down at once leaves the popup up); it moves to `keeping_popups`, and once the core has kept it its `herdr.popup` label goes. A keep the core turns down later brings it back as the popup (`reshow_popup`), unless another popup is up by then: that one stays, and the one nothing would show ends. One kept into a space that closed meanwhile ends with the space. Nothing ends a popup's terminal that is the person's beyond the popup (`held_elsewhere`): in a pane (kept, swapped in, given one by `hc keep` or another window), core-kept, or on its way into a pane (a keep made elsewhere counts from the next listing, within 500 ms); a kept wait popup keeps its terminal when its program ends. Closing or quitting the window ends the last popup and removes it and every wait popup's terminal not held so (`end_popups_now`, within the 250 ms close deadline shared with the rest of closing; what it misses the next start reaps). A hidden popup another client attached to (`hc attach`) ends with the popup too: the app can't see other clients, and a popup is the app's view of a program, not a place to come back to. Every popup terminal carries `herdr.popup=<app pid>` (`POPUP_LABEL`); a window's first listing ends and removes local, unkept ones whose app process is no longer running (`orphan_popups`, `process_running`), which is what a crash or forced quit leaves. Another live app process's popups are left alone. The process check sees this host's process view only, so an app in another process namespace (a container, Flatpak) sharing the core would have its popups taken for orphans; on Windows, which can't check a process here, none count as orphans. It replaces the logic of the `herdr-popup` shell script in dotfiles, which is a wrapper over it.

- `--hold` reads one key with `stty` and `dd`, which any `sh` has (dash, Linux's `/bin/sh`, has no `read -n`).
- Timeouts: the control server gives `terminal.popup` 15 s (`POPUP_ANSWER_WITHIN`), past the 10 s `Core::call` may take for `terminal.run` on the UI thread, and the CLI waits 2 s longer. If the CLI gives up anyway, the reply channel is gone when the window answers, and the window ends and removes the wait popup (`abandon_popup`) instead of keeping it.
- While a request waits, the server checks its stream for a hang-up every 500 ms (`await_reply`, `hung_up`): Ctrl-C on `--wait` drops the waiter, and the program keeps running. On Unix a hang-up is `POLLHUP` (asked for with `POLLOUT`, which macOS needs to report it), so a client that only shut its writing half after the line (`printf … | nc -U`) is still waited for and gets its answer. A finished wait popup nobody has asked about keeps its answer and its terminal for `WAIT_KEPT` (60 s), then both go (`expire_waits`); closing or quitting the window removes them (`end_popups_now`).
- `--size`: a side up to 1 is a fraction of the window and one from 50 is px; between them (`1.5`) is a usage error, more likely a mistyped fraction than a tiny popup.
- Files: `src/commands.rs`, `src/window.rs` (`popup`, `Popped`, `replace_popped`, `end_popped`, `held_elsewhere`, `reshow_popup`, `keeping_popups`, `forget_gone_popped`, `open_peek`, `close_overlay`, `"terminal.keep"`, `start_terminal_change`, `apply_terminal_change`, `settled`, `abandon_popup`, `end_terminal`, `wait_for`, `settle_waits`, `expire_waits`, `end_popups_now`, `POPUP_LABEL`, `orphan_popups`, `process_running`, `popup_hold_argv`, tests `popup_peeks`, `orphan_popups_are_those_of_apps_that_are_gone`), `src/control.rs` (`popup`, `parse_popup`, `exit_status`, `serve`, `await_reply`, `hung_up`, tests), `src/hc.rs` (`Core::start`, `Core::start_record`, `EventLines::ended`, test core `answering`), `src/main.rs`, `defaults/mods/peek/main.js`, `defaults/AGENTS.md`, `docs/commands.md`
- Upstream: none yet

### System notifications

`feat/system-notifications` (branched from `master`, fork only): an agent alert ("<name> needs you", "<name> hit an error", "<name> finished") also goes to the system's notification center through GPUI's `show_system_notification`, titled as the toast with the agent's own message (its 7501 `msg`, when it gave one) above "space / tab" as the body and tagged `herdr-agent:<address>`, so a newer alert for the same agent replaces the older one. `system_notifications` in settings.json says when: `background` (default; only while Herdr's window isn't key), `always`, or `off`; never while the person is looking at that agent (`alert::should_notify`). Clicking one activates Herdr and runs `agent.focus` for it, as the toast does; it is dismissed once the agent is looked at, starts working again or goes away, except a "needs you" one, which stays until the agent is no longer blocked (below). `dock_bounce: true` calls `request_attention` for "needs you" while Herdr is in the background. `alert_command` (a string run by `/bin/sh -c`, or an argv array) runs fire-and-forget in place of the system notification, under the same rule, with `HERDR_AGENT_STATE` (blocked|error|done), `HERDR_AGENT_PREV_STATE`, `HERDR_AGENT_APP`, `HERDR_AGENT_NAME`, `HERDR_AGENT_ADDRESS`, `HERDR_AGENT_PLACE`, `HERDR_ALERT_TITLE`, and the agent's own 7501 words as given (`HERDR_AGENT_MESSAGE` from `msg`, `HERDR_AGENT_KIND` from `kind`, `HERDR_AGENT_TITLE` from `title`; empty when absent, read from the terminal's record in the core's listing by `alert::Said::of`), so the herdr TUI's (text user interface's) and tuios's `notify` adapters have a Herdr counterpart. The vendored GPUI posts nothing unless the app runs from a bundle (its `NSInternalInconsistencyException` guard), so dev builds stay silent. Settings window: a "System notifications" choice and a "Bounce the Dock icon" toggle under Alerts.

- An `alert_command` starts in the home folder (the app's own is `/` when opened from the Finder) and in its own process group; still running after 30 s, the whole group is killed (`alert::reap`), so a hung one doesn't keep its thread and programs forever. One that doesn't start posts the system notification instead (`alert::Sent::Fallback`), and an error toast says why, once per distinct reason (`alert_command_errors`, cleared when a settings reload changes `alert_command`). A shell line always starts, so a missing program only shows as exit 127: one that started returns `alert::Sent::Ran` with a channel on which the reaper thread sends why a non-zero exit failed (`alert::exit_failure`), and the window then posts the system notification and the same once-per-reason toast. One killed by a signal is only logged at warn level.
- Sticky "needs you" (`notification_sticky`, on by default; settings.json only): `alert::Notified` records each posted agent notification with its alert, and `Notified::withdraw`, run on every listing in `tend_terminals`, decides when it comes down. A `Blocked` one stays while the agent's word is `blocked` or `error` (the states `Model::status` calls blocked and the Dock badge counts), however often the person looks at it, and goes once the agent works, idles, finishes or has no report, or its terminal goes (`Notified::gone`). A program that exits back to its shell loses its notification only once the core reports that change (a fake agent that exits without a last report kept the core's blocked report live); until then the agents list and Dock badge count it too. `Notified` lives in memory but the core outlives the app, so on the core's first listing after a start (`listed_once`, set by `terminals_listed`, not by a tick before it) `tend_terminals` adopts what may still be up, as the agent's state would have posted it: a blocked agent's as a "needs you", an error's as an error, and a finished one not yet seen (`Model::status` says done) as a finish, each then going as its kind does; any other agent's tag is dismissed (a no-op when none is up). A finish is known unseen only from the core's turn against the shared seen turn (the `herdr.seen` label): an agent no window has marked seen (no label) starts as idle in the new window, so its "finished" notification is dismissed on the first listing. Agents read off their screens (`harness.rs`, no 7501 reports) aren't adopted: their state is read again after a start, and a blocked or finished one notifies again, replacing the old notification under the same tag. A notification for a terminal that ended while Herdr was down stays: finding it would need the vendored GPUI to list delivered notifications. The late `alert_command` fallback also skips a terminal that has gone meanwhile. Errors, finishes, and every alert with the setting off go as before, once seen or back at work. An agent that goes idle or finishes unseen gets its "finished" alert, which replaces the needs-you one under the same tag, and one that goes back to work just loses it; one blocked again posts again under that tag, so its notifications never stack. Nothing posts for an agent the person is looking at when it blocks (`should_notify`, unchanged); the notification an `alert_command` that fails late falls back to posts only if the person isn't looking at the agent then and it still says what it alerted for (`alert::still_says`), so it never sticks while seen or replaces a newer one. Tests: `alert::tests::{a_needs_you_notification_stays_until_the_agent_is_no_longer_blocked, notified_agents_whose_terminals_are_gone_are_forgotten, notification_sticky_is_on_unless_set_off, a_late_alert_posts_only_while_the_agent_still_says_so}`, `window::tests::a_needs_you_notification_stays_until_the_agent_is_unblocked` (through `tend_terminals`).
- A status of `error` is its own alert (`model::Alert::Error`, `HERDR_AGENT_STATE=error`), rung, bounced and toasted as "needs you" is (`Alert::needs_you`), so an adapter can tell a stopped agent from one waiting on a question.
- Files: `src/alert.rs`, `src/model.rs` (`Alert`), `src/window.rs` (`alert`, `tend_terminals`, `notified`, the response handler in `AppWindow::new`), `src/settings.rs`, `src/main.rs`, `defaults/frame/settings/main.js`, `defaults/AGENTS.md`
- Upstream: pull request #634 (`feat/system-notifications-upstream`), awaiting review

### Notification sounds, marks, icons and `system.notify`

`feat/native-notify` (branched from `master`, fork only): agent notifications take the touches the person's `alert_command` adapter (`herdr-app-notify`) gave them, and any program can post one as Herdr.

- GPUI: `SystemNotification` has `subtitle`, `sound` (`SystemNotificationSound::{Silent, Default, Named}`; `Silent` by default) and `image` (a file attached as a `UNNotificationAttachment`, which the notification center moves into its own store), and derives `Default`. `App::system_notifications_allowed` says `Some(false)` when macOS won't show one (no app bundle, or authorization denied; read with `getNotificationSettingsWithCompletionHandler` when the center starts and again after each ask, so it trails a change in System Settings by one call). macOS sets `UNNotificationSound defaultSound` / `soundNamed:` and the subtitle, and `willPresentNotification` also asks for `Sound`, so a notification posted while Herdr is in front (`always`) plays too. Linux and Windows start the body with the subtitle and ignore the sound and image.
- `notification_sounds` (alert.rs `Look::of`, settings.rs `notification_sounds`): a sound per state or one for all; absent is silent. A name goes to `soundNamed:` as given (`Glass`, no extension). Apple documents only the app's `Library/Sounds` folders and its bundle for named sounds; tools on the same API (dataJAR's Notifier) document `/Library/Sounds` and `/System/Library/Sounds` names working, and macOS plays the default sound for a name it can't find. Not heard here: a test bundle can't be granted notifications without the person.
- `notification_marks` (off by default, so notifications carry no marks unless it is set): the agent's glyph (`AGENT_GLYPHS`, a table keyed by programs.json key, else the `app` without -code/-cli/-agent) before the title and the state's mark (✋ 🔴 ✅) before the body.
- `notification_icons` (on by default; `notification_icon.rs`): the agent's program icon drawn with resvg as the agents list draws it (a tinted glyph on its tile, or a picture), 192 px, cached per program key and a hash of the file and look under `<home>/cache/notification-icons/`, and copied into `outgoing/` for each notification, since the notification center moves the file it is given; copies older than 10 minutes that nothing took are swept, beyond 32 the oldest go, and a program's older drawings go when it is drawn again. The main thread only picks the icon (`Source::program`, from the thread-local programs table); reading, hashing, drawing, the sweep and the copy run on the background executor, and the notification posts when its copy is ready (`alert::show`). A notification posted or dismissed under the same tag meanwhile wins, so a late one never replaces a newer one or comes back after a dismissal. Only macOS shows an image; elsewhere no icon is picked, so nothing is read, drawn or copied. Verified on an isolated bundled instance: the attachment is made (no warning) and the copy leaves `outgoing/` as the request is added, while the caller's own `icon` file stays.
- `system.notify {title, body?, subtitle?, sound?, agent?, id?, urgent?, icon?}` (window.rs `system_notify`, alert.rs `parse_notify`): checked at the boundary (title required; text cut at 200/2000 characters with control characters and direction overrides dropped; `id`, `sound`, `agent`, `icon` refused when malformed). Tagged `herdr-notify:<id>` (a new tag each time without one), so the same id replaces; a click on one naming an `agent` runs `agent.focus` (`NotifyClicks`, the newest 64). `off` fails, `background` while Herdr is in front shows the toast instead unless `urgent`, and where macOS won't show it (no bundle, denied) it fails (`alert::can_post`), so a caller can fall back. A window that takes over 5 s to answer makes the caller fall back while the window still posts later: one duplicate.
- Files: `vendor/gpui-pre/src/platform.rs`, `vendor/gpui-pre-macos/src/system_notifications.rs`, `vendor/gpui-pre-linux/src/linux/system_notifications.rs`, `vendor/gpui-pre-windows/src/system_notifications.rs`, `vendor/gpui-pre/src/app/test_context.rs`, `vendor/gpui-pre/examples/system_notifications.rs`, `src/alert.rs`, `src/notification_icon.rs`, `src/settings.rs`, `src/commands.rs`, `src/window.rs`, `defaults/frame/settings/main.js`, `defaults/AGENTS.md`, `docs/commands.md`
- Upstream: none

### Off-screen terminal output

`perf/offscreen-no-redraw` (branched from `master`, fork only): a test, `output_off_screen_keeps_the_screen_and_redraws_nothing`, holding what `TerminalView::ingest` already does: output in a terminal not drawn in the window's current frame (another tab or space, a parked terminal, a hidden overlay) feeds its screen and still reports titles, but doesn't notify the window. The ~8 window draws a second the bench's `idle-busy-bg` shows come from the working agents' spinners in the tabs, pane header and agents mods (`spin(900)`, woken every 100 ms by gpui-kit's `spin_layer`), not from terminal output: GPUI marks every ancestor of a woken view dirty, so each spinner step runs `AppWindow::render` while terminal draws stay at 0.

- Files: `src/terminal.rs` (tests)
- Upstream: in pull request #721 (`up/changed-rows-only`), with "Rows kept while output scrolls", awaiting review

### Rows kept while output scrolls

`perf/changed-rows-only` (branched from `master`, fork only): a terminal row's drawing (backgrounds, shaped text, icons and sprites) is built again only when the row changed, also while output scrolls the screen. libghostty marks every row dirty when the screen scrolls (see "Row Identity" in its `render.h`), so `Vt::frame` read each one as a new row and `paint_grid`, which kept drawings by position, rebuilt the whole screen every frame an agent streamed output (the bench's `agent` scenario: ~1000 rows a second, ~56 a frame). `Vt::frame` keeps a dirty row's `Rc` when it reads the same as the row with that id last frame, and `keep_rows` finds a row's drawing by its `Rc` wherever it moved. Selection, colors and width are in the cells, so a row selected, recolored or resized reads differently and is built again; the cell, font, fonts and ligature setting still clear every drawing, and with ligatures the cursor's column is part of the key.

- Files: `src/vt.rs` (`Frame`, `Vt::frame`, test `a_row_stays_the_same_rc_only_while_it_reads_the_same`), `src/terminal.rs` (`PaintedRow`, `keep_rows`, `paint_grid`, test `only_rows_that_changed_are_built_again`)
- Upstream: pull request #721 (`up/changed-rows-only`), awaiting review

### Fonts mapped, scrollback at core's cap

`perf/memory-fonts-scrollback` (branched from `master`, fork only): two memory savings. Font files the home brings (`fonts/`, each mod's `fonts/`) are memory-mapped and handed to `add_fonts` borrowed (`map_font`), so CoreText reads glyphs straight from the file and the font pages stay clean and file-backed: the eight 2.5 MB JetBrainsMonoHerdr files left the heap (heap 55.0 → 34.8 MB, Malloc Large dirty 24 → 4 MB, the mapped fonts 48 KB resident each, 0 dirty). A font file truncated in place while Herdr runs would crash it; replacing the file is safe. The app's own terminal copy caps its scrollback at herdr-core's default `history_mb`, 5 MiB (`SCROLLBACK_MAX_BYTES`), not 10 MB: a full terminal costs ~4.7 MB, not ~9.4 MB. Lines past the core's history only lived here, until the terminal is attached again; a `history_mb` set above 5 in herdr-core's config isn't followed, since the hc protocol doesn't report it.

- Files: `src/settings.rs` (`load_home_fonts`, `map_font`), `src/vt.rs` (`SCROLLBACK_MAX_BYTES`, test `scrollback_keeps_the_latest_styled_lines_within_its_byte_cap`), `Cargo.toml` (`memmap2`, already in the lock through fontdb)
- Upstream: none yet

### Hidden terminals free their drawing caches

`perf/hidden-caches` (branched from `master`, fork only): a terminal off screen for a few seconds frees what drawing kept for it. Every tab's terminals stay attached (instant switches, kitty images), and each one ever drawn held libghostty's render state (its row cache) and the app's `Cell` rows and row drawings: ~2.4 MB of heap per terminal at the bench's window size. A drawn terminal checks every `HIDDEN_CHECK` (2 s) whether the window's last frame showed it (`shown_in`, the signal off-screen output already uses); off screen for two checks in a row (2 to 4 s), `TerminalView::release_frame_caches` drops the row drawings and `Vt::release_frame_caches` frees the render state and makes a new one (libghostty has no reset call) and forgets the rows read from it, then the check stops until the terminal is drawn again, which reads the whole screen as a tab switch already did. Quick flips keep everything. The screen, scrollback, selection, scroll position and kitty images stay. A synchronized update in progress keeps its held frame (no release then), and a released terminal doesn't fill the render state for one. `Vt::new` frees the state it filled to read the default colors, so terminals attached in hidden tabs (all of them after a restart) hold none until drawn.

- Measured (isolated headless app, agent-deck layout, 31 fake agents, 1708×1398 window): heap at 31 terminals 116–120 → 44–50 MB; footprint 278–280 → 200–212 MB (the freed pages turn reclaimable); the render-state row cache 49.2 → 1.6 MB and the `Cell` rows 24.5 → 0.8 MB (malloc_history). Worst frame in the second after a tab switch p50 22–25 ms either way, also into a released tab (22–24 ms); steady streaming CPU (central processing unit) unchanged (311–318 vs 335 M instructions/s).
- Limit: a released terminal (never drawn, or freed while hidden) keeps no held frame, so one first shown while its program is inside a synchronized update draws that update half done on its first frame, rather than the frame before it, until the update ends or upstream's one-second deadline releases it. Upstream's `synchronized_output_releases_at_its_deadline` reads a frame first for this reason.
- Files: `src/vt.rs` (`Vt::release_frame_caches`, `Shared::released`, `on_render_hold`, `Vt::new`, test `released_frame_caches_read_the_same_frame_again`), `src/terminal.rs` (`HIDDEN_CHECK`, `watch_hidden`, `release_frame_caches`, `drawing` for tests), `src/window.rs` (test `a_hidden_terminal_frees_its_drawing_and_draws_the_same_when_shown`)
- Upstream: pull request #546 (`perf/release-hidden-terminal-caches`), awaiting review

### Spinners turn without a draw

`perf/spinner-repaint-only` (branched from `master`, fork only): a mod's `svg(...).spin(ms)` turns with no render, layout or paint of anything, its view's or the window's. GPUI marks every ancestor of a notified view dirty and renders the root view on every draw, so the 100 ms wake gpui-kit's `spin_layer` gave its ShellRoot ran `AppWindow::render` and the whole window's prepaint and paint per step (the bench's `idle-busy-bg`: ~8 window draws a second with no terminal drawn). GPUI has spinning sprites: `Svg::spinning(period)` / `Window::paint_spinning_svg` paint a monochrome sprite whose `pad` slot (which no shader reads) holds its period, so a cached view replaying the sprite replays the spin. `Window::present` turns every spinning sprite of the frame on screen to `gpui::spin_clock_ms()`, and while that frame has one, a timer asks for a present-only frame every `SPIN_STEP` (100 ms, ~10 fps) through the platform's frame source. gpui-kit's `spin` draws such an svg in place, so the hosted spinner layer (`Spin`, `spin_layer`, `build_view`, `Fade` and the inline fallbacks) is gone, and a spinner keeps its true stacking, opacity and clipping. A spinner turns about its drawn sprite's center (within a device pixel of its element's center).

- A step already scheduled when a draw took the spinners away presents nothing; steps wait while the window is hidden (`Window::visibility`) and start again when it is visible.
- `Scene` keeps `has_spinners`, set when a spinning sprite is inserted (cached replay inserts too) and cleared with the scene, so a present without spinners skips the scan.
- `Svg::spinning` works for an `external_path` or inline data svg as well as a `path` one.
- A spinner drawn fully transparent (opacity or colour alpha 0) is left out of the frame, so it doesn't keep the window presenting. `spin(ms)` ignores a non-finite `ms` and clamps the rest to 100 ms – 1 hour; the gpui-kit tests sit outside the workspace, so a root-crate test (`spin_with_an_absurd_period_draws_without_panicking`) hands Infinity, NaN and 1e300 to `spin` through the script bridge (`__apply`; `.spin()` itself rejects them in JavaScript) and renders.
- The turn is not reflected in the sprite's bounds for layer ordering and clipping, so a spinner should be round or symmetric within its box.

- Files: `vendor/gpui-pre/src/scene.rs` (`MonochromeSprite::spins`, `turn`, `Scene::turn_spinners`, `has_spinners`), `vendor/gpui-pre/src/window.rs` (`SPIN_STEP`, `spin_clock_ms`, `paint_spinning_svg`, `present`, `schedule_spin_step`, `refresh_visibility`, `presented_spinners`, `presents`), `vendor/gpui-pre/src/elements/svg.rs` (`Svg::spinning`), `vendor/gpui-pre/src/app/test_context.rs` (`set_asset_source`, `simulate_window_frame_request`), `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/materialize.rs`, `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/root.rs`, `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/view.rs`, `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/typings.rs`, `src/window.rs` (comment), `src/mods/lifecycle_tests.rs` (test `spinners_turn_without_drawing_the_window`)
- Upstream: none yet. Upstream's unmerged `fix/spinner-frame-clock` (cdba8fa, ffe8d0f) swaps the 100 ms wake for `window.request_animation_frame()`, which keeps the full window draw per step and steps it every display frame; this replaces the wake with present-only frames instead.

### Frames step without a draw

`perf/frames-present-only` (branched from `master`, fork only): a mod's `.frames(list, ms)` text spinner steps with no render, layout or paint, as `spin` does (Spinners turn without a draw). gpui-kit notified the spinner's view at every step, so each frame was a window render and a mod rebuild: a simulated second of braille at 80 ms gave 16 window renders, 16 mod rebuilds and 15 presents; this branch gives 0, 0 and 12 or 13 presents (one per frame), with 0 script renders before and after.

- GPUI frame cycles: `FrameCycle { index, count, period_ms }` packs into a sprite's spare `u32` (`MonochromeSprite::frame`, which was the alignment field `contrast_pad`, and `PolychromeSprite::pad`; no shader reads either, and the WGSL (WebGPU Shading Language) and HLSL (High-Level Shading Language) structs keep the old name by position). `Window::with_frame_cycle` tags the glyph and emoji sprites `paint_glyph` and `paint_emoji` paint. Each present (`Scene::turn_spinners`) shows the sprites of the frame `spin_clock_ms` is on at their tile's size and hides the rest at zero size; cached-view replay copies the sprites as painted, so a replayed cycle keeps cycling.
- Step timer: `turn_spinners` returns when (on the spin clock) the next spinner step or frame boundary is due, and `schedule_spin_step` presents then; a sooner step replaces a later one already scheduled (`spin_step_due`), so a slow cycle never holds back a fast one drawn after it, and a superseded timer does nothing.
- Inside a cycle glyphs draw grayscale (subpixel sprites have no spare field), so a spinner on an opaque window draws a little lighter than subpixel text beside it. A frame drawn fully transparent is left out, and a list of one frame draws plainly, so neither keeps the window presenting. Periods are at least 16 ms in GPUI.
- gpui-kit: `frames` builds `FramesText` (`materialize.rs`): every frame shaped once in the inherited text style, stacked and centred in the widest one's box, each painted in its cycle; at most 255 frames, `ms` 16 to 65535. `apply_loops` keeps `.loop()` only. Only glyphs take turns: an underline or background paints under every frame. Frames follow `spin_clock_ms`, so they step with the ring.
- Tests: `mods::lifecycle_tests::{frames_step_without_drawing_the_window (0 renders, 0 rebuilds, a present per 80 ms frame, the frame shown advances), a_faster_spinner_drawn_later_steps_at_its_own_pace, emoji_frames_cycle_and_invisible_frames_are_left_out}`, on real CoreText (`TestAppContext::build_with_text_system` with `MacTextSystem`, macOS only). 16 mutations; 14 caught. Not caught: dropping the grayscale guard (the test platform has no subpixel rendering) and dropping the superseded-timer check (costs one extra present).
- Files: `vendor/gpui-pre/src/scene.rs` (`FrameCycle`, `turn_spinners`, `show_sprite`, `has_spinners`), `vendor/gpui-pre/src/window.rs` (`with_frame_cycle`, `paint_glyph`, `paint_emoji_with_color`, `spin_due`, `spin_step_due`, `schedule_spin_step`, `presented_frames`), `vendor/gpui-pre-apple/vendor/gpui/src/scene.rs` (field rename), `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/materialize.rs` (`FramesText`, `apply_loops`), `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/typings.rs`, `defaults/AGENTS.md`, `src/mods/lifecycle_tests.rs`
- Upstream: none yet

### Spinner steps present from their timer

`perf/idle-456` (branched from `master`, fork only): a spinner or frame-cycle step (Spinners turn without a draw, Frames step without a draw) with nothing else to draw presents from its own timer on macOS while the window's display link is parked, instead of waking the link. The link parks after three idle vsyncs (`step` in `vendor/gpui-pre-macos/src/window.rs`), so every step restarted it: each `CVDisplayLinkStart` spawns a CoreVideo thread and re-reads display timing, the step then waited for the link's first callback, and the link ran three more callbacks before parking again. Core Animation shows a drawable presented between link callbacks at the next vsync, so `schedule_spin_step`'s timer calls `present()` itself when the platform says it may (`PlatformWindow::presents_between_frames`: on macOS, while the link is parked; `start_display_link` clears `parked`; false on every other platform) and the window is neither dirty nor waiting on next-frame callbacks. Otherwise it asks for a frame as before, so a running link (output, input, a draw) presents the step on its next callback and presents never outrun the display. A step timer whose due time a draw has since moved later reschedules instead of presenting, on either path.

- Measured (idle in the person's shape: an isolated home copied from theirs, their mods, layout and settings, an isolated core, eight idle terminals and one reporting working with a ◐/◑ title once a second, window behind other windows with the bench's draw-when-covered switch, screen locked but on, 60 s per run, 5 interleaved rounds of `master` / `master` with the bench counters / branch, medians [min–max]): app wakeups 66.5 / 66.4 / 18.1 a second [18.0–18.7]; CVDisplayLink threads in a 10 s `sample` 113 [106–123] / 108 [106–121] / 0; display link starts 11.4 / 0.08 a second; app instructions 48.5 / 49.4 / 42.4 M/s; app CPU 2.2% [1.95–2.8] / 2.47 [1.93–2.73] / 1.7 [1.18–1.92]; spinner steps 12.58 a second on both; window draws 0.12 to 0.13 a second on all three. Busy samples over five 10 s samples (first round of the same A/B) 994 on `master` / 438 on the branch: on `master` 49% were the display link (thread starts, timing re-reads and its idle callbacks), on the branch none; the present itself (encode, Metal submit, Core Animation) is about 0.45% of a core either way.
- Step timing, at the `present()` call on the spin clock, not on screen: a timer present then waits for the next vsync (up to one refresh interval), which a present inside a link callback has already done. Mean lateness 24.4 ms [22.1–24.8] / 6.4 ms [3.5–6.6]; the worst step in a run 43 [37–45] / 33 [20–39] ms. Over about 16 minutes of branch runs one step was 396 ms late (in the first A/B, before the parked gate), none other over 50 ms; base's worst was 45 ms.
- Bench (`scripts/bench.py` default set and `--set spinners`, 12 s per scenario, 5 interleaved rounds per side against `master` with the counters, none dropped; Minstr/s app, core and holder): at rest equal. Spinners with nothing drawing (spinners, spinners+title, idle-busy-bg): link starts 7.8 → 0.1–0.2 a second, wakeups about 60 → 30, steps 7.8 → 9.4 a second (each was 28 ms late on `master`, so steps came about every 128 ms), lateness 28.5 → 5.5–6.6 ms. Streaming (agent, four-panes, ten-agents, spinners+stream): presents 23 → 18 a second with window draws equal, since a step timer a draw had superseded no longer asks for a present; link starts 4 → 6 a second, as those frames had kept the link awake; instructions 3–4% lower (agent 212 → 204, spinners+stream 281 → 272). tui and spinners+tui: presents 15 → 10 a second, draws equal, lateness about 9.5 ms either way.
- Bench counters (`HERDR_APP_BENCH`, `scripts/bench.py`): `presents`, `spin_steps`, `spin_late_ms` and `spin_late_max_ms` (how late a due step reached a present; lateness of a second or more, a step held back while the window was hidden, isn't counted, so a stall that long doesn't show in the max), `link_starts` (`gpui::frame_counts`, counted in `Window::present` and `display_link::subscribe`).
- Tests: `mods::lifecycle_tests::spinner_steps_present_without_waking_the_frame_source` (five steps present from the timer, each turning the spinner, with no frame source wake and no window render; a draw between steps then one present; a frame presented between steps makes the timer it superseded present nothing and the next one step; without the platform's leave a step wakes the frame source). Mutations: dropping the timer present, its re-scheduling, the platform check or the superseded-step guard are caught; dropping the dirty check or the next-frame-callback check are not (test mode draws a notified window at once, so a step never finds it dirty; next-frame callbacks wake the frame source when added). `parked` itself is macOS state the test platform doesn't model.
- Files: `vendor/gpui-pre/src/window.rs` (`schedule_spin_step`, `present`), `vendor/gpui-pre/src/platform.rs` (`presents_between_frames`), `vendor/gpui-pre-macos/src/window.rs` (`presents_between_frames`, `start_display_link`), `vendor/gpui-pre-macos/src/display_link.rs`, `vendor/gpui-pre/src/frame_counts.rs`, `vendor/gpui-pre/src/gpui.rs`, `vendor/gpui-pre/src/platform/test/window.rs`, `vendor/gpui-pre/src/app/test_context.rs` (`set_window_presents_between_frames`, `window_frame_wakes`), `src/bench.rs`, `scripts/bench.py`, `src/mods/lifecycle_tests.rs`
- Upstream's three questions: (1) Does the app act on it? It is GPUI's frame pacing, below the app. (2) Can only the app see it? Yes: the platform window and its display link. (3) Can a mod not do it? Yes: mods never see frames. Verdict: D (fork only). It rests on the fork's present-only spinner steps and its display link parking, neither of which upstream Herdr or Zed's GPUI has; it could go to Zed's GPUI only together with link parking.
- Not done, measured: re-encoding only the spinners' sprites per step (partial present), on CPU grounds only (GPU and WindowServer cost not measured): the CPU encode is about 1.1 ms a second (54 `render_frame` samples in 50 s, about 0.1% of a core) and the rest of the present (Metal submit, Core Animation) would stay, while a partial present needs the last frame kept in a texture of its own and blitted each step, a renderer change across backends. Deferred: reusing the draw-order tree (`BoundsTree`, which orders a scene's primitives; the idle profile called it hit testing) across unchanged draws, since idle draws are rare in this shape (0.12 a second, 0 samples in 50 s); it is about 7.6% of draw time when the window draws.

### Toasts coalesced and capped

`perf/toasts` (branched from `master`, fork only): agent alerts of one kind (finished, needs you, hit an error) within `ALERT_JOIN` (3 s) of the last share one toast: "3 agents finished", names newest first, four named and the rest counted, each agent once; a click runs `agent.focus` on the newest. One agent's toast is not joined. The joined toast is redrawn in place (`mods::set_props` swaps the copy's props, `runtime.refresh` re-renders it) and its timeout restarts, so a wave loads one `toast` mod instead of one per agent. At most `TOAST_LIMIT` (5) toasts show, for any source (`notify`, alerts, errors): opening a sixth closes the oldest routine toast, and errors and warnings (`level` error or warning, which includes needs-you alerts; `Overlay::urgent`) only once no routine one is left, so a wave of finished toasts can't push out an unread error. The toast just opened is never the one closed (`feat/toast-prefs`): with five errors showing, a new toast closes the oldest error rather than itself. Toast `store-*.json` files are named by a hash of the props but only written when a mod uses localStorage, which the shipped `toast` mod doesn't, so they don't pile up. Herdr's own toasts expire on the window's 400 ms `poll`. A mod root's toast clock running only while it shows a toast is upstream's (#548).

- Measured with the perf probe's `load.py` (headless, agent-deck, ~30 fake agents) on a loaded machine, best-of-two per side, so read instruction counts over CPU %: status storm (~27 "finished" alerts a second) 3045 → ~1415 M instructions/s, ~994k → ~336k allocations/s, materialize passes 775 → ~26 per probe, `open_overlay` 10–18% of main-thread busy time → 0, frame p50 7.8 → ~1.6 ms, p95 36.7 → 24.6–42.5 ms; done wave (15 agents within 1 s) 575 → ~436 M instructions/s, p95 15.3 → 8.7–14.5 ms; idle wake-ups 92 → ~73 a second. Child-process CPU (~30% in the storm) is not toast work and did not move.
- Tests: `an_alert_toast_names_one_agent_and_counts_more`, `alert_toasts` (a wave joins and redraws one toast, another kind or a later alert gets its own, the cap keeps the newest); `alert_errors` expects the joined toast.
- Files: `src/window.rs` (`TOAST_LIMIT`, `ALERT_JOIN`, `ALERT_NAMES`, `toast_timeout`, `AlertToast`, `AlertedAgent`, `Overlay::alert`, `Overlay::urgent`, `alert`, `join_alert_toast`, `close_toasts_past_limit`, `open_overlay`, tests), `src/mods.rs` (`LIVE_PROPS`, `set_props`, `props`), `src/alert.rs` (`title`, `merged_title`), `defaults/AGENTS.md`
- Upstream: the lazy `ShellRoot` toast clock merged upstream as #548 (`cfcf836`) and is upstream's code here; the alert coalescing and cap are fork only.

### Mod reads share one copy

`perf/mod-render` (branched from `master`, fork only): an Agent Deck redraw (a status change, the idle frames) took ~21 ms, ~13 of it in the mods' scripts, and about half of that was host reads: every `panes()`, `agents()`, `terminals()` call converted the whole list `serde_json::Value` → `HostValue` → JS, and `keys()` read keys.json and looked for every mod a binding names, each call. Snapshot reads (`reader`) hand gpui-kit a `HostValue::Json(Arc<Value>)` copied once per revision (`shared_part`), which the bridge turns into JS straight from the shared value (`json_into_js`); each call still returns a fresh copy, so a mod that mutates what it read sees no difference (~25% less per read: 30 panes 58 → 45 µs, 30 terminals 168 → 122 µs). `keys()` is listed again only when keys.json or the mods folder changes (`keys_part`, 355 → ~125 µs). The shipped `tabs` mod reads `panes()` once per render instead of twice per tab, as upstream's #485 and #486 do for `agents` and `worktree`; `defaults/AGENTS.md` tells mods to read lists once per render. A cached JSON text parsed with `JSON.parse` was tried first and was slower than building the objects (QuickJS's JSON string parser).

- Measured with the perf probe's `load.py` (headless agent-deck, 30 fake agents, two runs per side, a heavily loaded machine: read script time and instructions over wall time). Script time per mod frame (p50): idle 12.9 → 6.8 ms, status storm 14.6 → 7.8 ms; mod-frame p50 idle 21.6 → 16.6 ms, storm 25.8 → 18.9 ms; app instructions idle 166 → 137, storm 740 → 603 M/s. Most of the rest is GPUI layout, prepaint and materialize of the panels' trees (~6–8 ms), the agents list above all.
- Files: `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/host_modules.rs` (`HostValue::Json`), `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/engine/quickjs/host_modules.rs` (`json_into_js`, test), `src/mods.rs` (`shared_part`, `keys_part`, `cached_part`, tests), `defaults/mods/tabs/main.js`, `defaults/AGENTS.md`

### Interface zoom and text size

`feat/ui-zoom-text-scale` (branched from `master`, fork only): two settings.json scales for the panels, both default 1, where 1 draws exactly as `zoom` alone. `ui_zoom` (0.75 to 1.5) scales the layout's px sizes, pane headers, tabs, overlays, popups, toasts and every mod's px on top of `zoom`; terminals keep their font, cells, letter spacing and padding at `zoom` alone. `ui_text_scale` (0.8 to 1.3) multiplies only text: Herdr's panel text (`settings::zt`, `settings::text_rems` for what was `text_xs`, and the window root's text size), and mods' `text_size(...)` (px or `"1rem"`), the reflected `text_xs()` … `text_3xl()`, px and rem `line_height`s, editor `font_size`, mod tooltips and the shell's failure banner through a `HERDR:` text scale in gpui-kit's bridge (`set_text_scale`, `scaled_text_px`, `scaled_text_rems`, `style::text_length`, `style::apply_nullary`, `style::line_height`). The window's rem (`settings::ui_rem`) is the base text size at `zoom × ui_zoom` without the text scale, so rem spacing, radii and gpui-kit's own panels keep their size. `zoom × ui_zoom` stops at the largest zoom (3). Both are clamped when settings.json is read, reported by `Settings::problems()` when out of range (`asked_ui_scales`), and apply live like the other settings. Mods get `settings().ui_scale` (`zoom × ui_zoom`, capped) and `settings().ui_scale_ranges` (`Settings::for_mods`); mods that turn window px back into their own (`tabs`, `picker`, Herdr 98's `kit.js` and taskbar, What's New) divide by `ui_scale`; Herdr 98's terminal font stays on `zoom`. Rust chrome that used rem spacing (`p_2`, `px_3`, the exit badge) uses `z()` instead, so text scale leaves its spacing alone.

- Settings window: "Interface zoom" and "Interface text size" steppers (5% steps) under Zoom on the Text > Zoom page (the "Interface font" is on Text > Fonts), from the value in use (settings.json's as Herdr clamps it) within the published ranges (`scaleStep`); 100% leaves the key out of settings.json.
- Tests: `cargo test ui_scale` matches only the inner `mod_ui_scales`, which returns at once outside its own process; `mods_follow_ui_zoom_and_text_scale_live` is the one that runs it.

### Screen scale for mods

`feat/mod-scale-factor` (branched from `master`, fork only): `window().scale_factor`, the backing scale of the screen the window is on (GPUI's `Window::scale_factor`: 1, or 2 on Retina), so a mod can size or round to whole device pixels per screen. It sits in `window()`, not `settings()`, because it belongs to the window rather than settings.json. `window_json` reports it. A move to a screen of another scale makes GPUI call `bounds_changed`, which redraws the window; `render` republishes `window()` and redraws the copies that read it, as it does for `maximized`. Muster's peek uses it to pick its text size per screen.

- Files: `src/window/titlebar.rs` (`window_json`), `src/window.rs` (test `mod_scale_factor`), `defaults/herdr.d.ts`, `defaults/AGENTS.md`

### Background launch for tests

`HERDR_APP_BACKGROUND` (any value) opens the main window without focus and skips `cx.activate`, so a test or benchmark instance starts behind the person's windows instead of taking the keyboard; `scripts/bench.py` sets it. A window manager rule can then place it (the bare binary's app name is `herdr-app`, distinct from the bundle's `Herdr`). macOS doesn't draw windows on a hidden space, so drawing measurements and captures still need a visible one.

- Files: `src/main.rs` (`open_main`), `scripts/bench.py`
- Upstream: none yet

### Bench draws covered windows

`perf/bench-draw-occluded` (branched from `master`, fork only): `HERDR_BENCH_DRAW_OCCLUDED=1`, read once at launch, makes macOS treat every window as presented. The display link keeps running and the window reports `Visible` while covered, so a benchmark instance that opens behind the person's windows (Background launch for tests) still draws and steps its spinners. Without it, a fully covered window draws nothing: an earlier burst A/B measured 0 window renders a second and so compared terminal processing, not drawing. `scripts/bench.py` sets it. Any other value, or none, leaves occlusion in charge as upstream, and the shipped app never sets it. A minimised window or one on another Space also keeps drawing under the switch, so it is for benchmarks only.

- GPUI: `presented` in `vendor/gpui-pre-macos/src/window.rs` is the one occlusion check, used by `start_display_link`, `visibility` and `window_did_change_occlusion_state`.
- Tests: `gpui-pre-macos` `window::tests::occluded_windows_stop_drawing_unless_the_bench_switch_is_exactly_1` (the crate sits outside the workspace, so run it with `cargo test --manifest-path vendor/gpui-pre-macos/Cargo.toml`, not `just check`). Checked on screen: a covered burst instance draws with the switch and doesn't without it.

### Pane arrangements

`feat/pane-arrange` and `feat/auto-arrange` (branched from `master`, fork only): `pane.arrange {mode, tab?}` rebuilds a tab's existing panes (default: the shown tab) into a preset shape with even sizes, as tmux's `select-layout` and tuios's tiling modes do. Modes: `tiled` (grid, `ceil(sqrt(n))` across, row by row, a short last row's panes widen: tmux's tiled), `bsp` (dwindle: each pane takes half of what's left, splitting right, down, right…), `master` (the focused pane on the left half, the rest stacked on the right), `columns`, `rows`, `next` (the mode after the tab's last one, from `tiled`) and `off` (below). Panes keep their block whole (id, terminal or mod, name), so nothing restarts; only the row/column containers are new, and the tree is saved and synced like a manual split. Order is reading order (by top edge, then left edge, from the current sizes), which every mode's output preserves, so cycling never shuffles panes except that `master` moves the focused pane first. Focus stays; a zoomed pane unzooms. The tab's mode is this screen's choice (`TabView.arrange` in the saved view, not the shared document) and shows to mods as the tab's `arrange`. Keys: `cmd-shift-space` (macOS) and `ctrl-shift-space` (Linux, Windows) run `pane.arrange:next`; existing untouched key files get it through upstream's shipped-originals upgrade (f524898), edited ones need the line added. The palette lists "Next pane arrangement" and each mode.

- Auto-arrange: a tab keeps its mode (yabai's per-space layout), so every pane split into it or closed in it (except in `bsp`, below), by any path (`pane.split`, `pane.close`, a mod pane, a terminal kept or adopted for an agent, a program that ends), runs the mode again with even sizes. The hook is in `Model::split_tree` (used by `split_with` and by the window's initial-size guess) and `Model::remove_pane`, which own the tab's view state; a document arriving from another screen replaces the tree without them, so screens with different modes never re-arrange each other's changes. Order is the tab's reading order before the change, the new pane inserted right after the pane it split and a closed pane dropped (`arrange::arrange_in`). `bsp` keeps its tree instead (`Mode::rebuilds_on_change`), as tuios's `BSPTree.InsertWindow`/`RemoveWindow` and bspwm do: `pane.arrange bsp` builds the dwindle once, then a split halves the focused pane in the direction asked (`ctrl-b |` right, `ctrl-b -` down) as a pair of its own (`Node::split_pair`: no merging into a parent running the same way, unless the target is alone in it), so a close collapses that pair and the space goes back to the pane it was split with, and dragged sizes stay. A pane dragged to another's edge pairs up with it the same way (`Model::move_pane`). Re-running the dwindle on every split threw the requested direction away. A pane added with no direction (a terminal adopted for an agent) takes `Model::auto_axis`: across its parent (bspwm's spiral), so several in a row dwindle instead of thinning out side by side. Re-arranging never picks a master, so `master` keeps the pane in its master slot; only `pane.arrange master` makes the focused pane master. Focus and zoom are the plain split's and close's (a split focuses the new pane and unzooms). A dragged divider holds until the next split or close; `pane.swap` and pane drags keep the mode, and the next arrangement goes on in the new order. A tab holding blocks other than panes splits and closes plainly. `pane.arrange off` (palette "Arrange panes: off") clears the mode and leaves the panes where they are; `next` never picks it. `default_arrange` in settings.json (a mode name; absent or anything else: none) gives tabs made on this screen (`Model::add_tab`, `add_space`) that mode; the Settings window's Appearance > Panes page sets it ("New tabs arrange").
- Files: `src/arrange.rs` (modes, `reading_order`, `arrange`, `arrange_in`, `check`, tests), `src/model.rs` (`Tab::arrange`, `Model::arrange_tab`, `split_tree`, `remove_pane`, `default_arrange`, tab JSON `arrange`, tests), `src/mirror.rs` (`TabView.arrange`, test), `src/settings.rs` (`Person::default_arrange`, test), `src/window.rs` (dispatch incl. `off`, `default_arrange` read in `reload_config`, document check, tests `arrange_panes` and `auto_arrange`), `src/commands.rs`, `src/main.rs`, `defaults/keys.json`, `defaults/keys.macos.json`, `defaults/frame/palette/main.js`, `defaults/frame/settings/main.js`, `scripts/settings.test.ts`, `defaults/herdr.d.ts`, `defaults/AGENTS.md`, `docs/commands.md`
- Tests: `arrange::tests::a_given_order_wins_over_reading_order_and_missing_panes_follow`; `model::tests::bsp_splits_the_pane_asked_in_the_direction_asked_and_keeps_its_tree` (direction kept, pairs, a close giving the space to its pair, drag kept, `auto_axis` dwindling), `a_tab_with_a_mode_arranges_again_when_a_pane_comes_or_goes` (columns insertion, close, drag kept until the next close, swap, plain without a mode), `master_mode_keeps_its_master_and_tiled_fills_the_grid_as_panes_come`, `new_tabs_start_in_the_default_mode`; `window::tests::auto_arrange` in its own process (split, close, drag, swap, bsp keeping its tree, `off`, `default_arrange` read from settings.json, the arranged tree in the shared document).
- Upstream: none yet. Upstream asks for an issue before growing the core (a command plus a tab field); the change uses nothing fork-only, but its palette and AGENTS.md lines sit beside fork-only ones, so a pull request is a cherry-pick onto `upstream/master` with those neighbours resolved.

### Focus follows mouse

`feat/focus-follows-mouse` (branched from `master`, fork only): `focus_follows_mouse` in settings.json (default off) focuses a terminal pane, a tab's or a layout's terminal block, once the pointer rests on it for `focus_follows_mouse_delay_ms` (0 to 2000, default 150; clamped when read), through `pane.focus` as a click would. The delay is a debounce: it runs from after the pointer rests, every real move starts it over, so sweeping across a pane on the way somewhere else doesn't focus it; at 0 ms the first move onto a new pane focuses it at once. Only a real pointer move counts: content scrolling or panes moving under a still pointer, and moves under 1 px (jitter, which neither starts nor delays a wait), keep the focus. After any key down, or a focus change made some other way (menu-bound keys, clicks, mods), a wait may only start once the pointer has gone more than 4 logical px from where it was then, so a pane the keyboard focused keeps the focus while the hand rests on the mouse. It never moves while a button is held (selections, pane and divider drags, link clicks), while a dialog, popup, peek or menu is open (toasts and window overlays don't count), while Herdr isn't the active window, or onto the pane already focused; a press, the pointer leaving the pane or the window, or any of those arriving cancel the wait. `focus.toggle_follows_mouse` flips the setting in settings.json and says so in a toast. Keys pressed while it is off hold the pointer too, so a key that turns it on keeps the focus where it is until the pointer moves 4 px. `write_person` returns `PersonSaved` (saved, broken or write failed); when it isn't saved the toast is a warning, "Focus follows mouse: on (not saved: settings.json has errors)" or "… (not saved: cannot write settings.json)", and a control-socket caller gets that as an error (the zoom keys apply unsaved). The rules are a pure `Follow` state machine (`still`, `moved`, `keys`, `fire`, `cancel`) the window feeds from its root's mouse-move, mouse-exit, mouse-down and key-down listeners; the pane under the pointer is the shown terminal block (the active tab's, zoom applied, or the layout's) whose painted bounds hold the pointer, unless a floating block or a window overlay is over it there, checked again against the pointer's position when the wait ends (hidden blocks keep stale bounds and terminal hover, so neither is trusted alone).

- Settings window: a "Focus follows mouse" toggle on Input > Mouse, and a "Focus delay" stepper (50 ms steps) shown while it's on. Off and 150 ms leave their keys out of settings.json.
- Files: `src/window/follow_mouse.rs` (`Follow`, `Gate`, `Step`, the window's `follow_mouse`, `follow_mouse_fire`, `toggle_follows_mouse`, `terminal_pane_at`, tests), `src/window.rs` (fields, root listeners, `focus.toggle_follows_mouse`, `write_person`/`change_person`, tests `follow_mouse`, `follow_mouse_shown` and `follow_beside_window`), `src/settings.rs` (`Person`, `focus_follows_mouse_delay`, tests), `src/commands.rs`, `defaults/frame/settings/main.js`, `defaults/frame/palette/main.js`, `defaults/herdr.d.ts`, `defaults/AGENTS.md`, `docs/commands.md`

### Zoom in whole points

`feat/zoom-point-step` (branched from `master`, fork only): `zoom_step` in settings.json, `"tenth"` (default, unchanged) or `"point"`. At `"point"` each press of the zoom keys sets `zoom` to `(points ± 1) / font_size`, so the terminals' text lands on whole points as Ghostty's `font-size` steps do (12, 13, 14, 15) instead of 13.2, 14.4, 15.6, which rasterise and round into the cell grid differently; the panels scale by the same factor. From between points it goes to the nearest whole point in the press's direction. The ends are the whole points inside the zoom range (0.5 to 3), and the zoom is clamped to that range after the division, so settings.json is never refused. `terminal_font_size` rounds a size within 0.01 of a whole point to it at `"point"`, since `points / size * size` can miss by an ulp (unit in the last place: 6.5 pt pressed to 7 gives 7.0000005); `"tenth"` keeps `font_size × zoom` as is. A first start's zoom (`first_zoom`) stays in tenths: a fresh settings.json has no `zoom_step`. A layout's own `font_size` is the size stepped from; changing it keeps `zoom`, so the text can sit between points until the next press. A `font_size` outside 6 to 72 (reported by `problems`, still drawn) steps in tenths, since at 0 or below there is no whole point to land on.

- Settings window: a "Zoom steps" picker (10% / Whole points) under Zoom on the Text > Zoom page; at whole points the Zoom stepper shows the terminals' text in points.
- Tests: `settings::tests::{zoom_steps_default_to_tenths, point_steps_land_the_terminal_text_on_whole_points, point_steps_from_between_points_go_to_the_nearest_one, point_steps_stay_inside_the_zoom_range, point_steps_fall_back_to_tenths_outside_the_font_size_range, tenth_steps_leave_the_text_size_unrounded}`, `mods::render_tests::zoom_row_shows_points_at_whole_point_steps`
- Files: `src/settings.rs` (`ZoomStep`, `Person`, `Settings::compose`, `Settings::next_zoom`, `terminal_font_size`), `defaults/frame/settings/main.js` (`ZOOM_STEPS`), `src/mods/render_tests.rs`, `defaults/AGENTS.md`

### Settings window keeps a broken settings.json

`fix/feature-review` (branched from `master`, fork only): the Settings window's `change` reads settings.json, edits it and writes it back only if nobody changed it meanwhile (`writeTextFile`'s `expected`), up to three times. While settings.json doesn't parse (`broken`, set by `load`), it writes nothing and an error toast says "settings.json has errors: fix it first", so one toggle can't replace the file with `{}` plus that one setting. When all three writes fail, an error toast says so with the last error. settings.json that parses to something other than a plain object (null, an array, a number) counts as broken too, and when reading it or applying the edit throws, an error toast titled "Cannot read settings.json" gives the reason.

- Files: `defaults/frame/settings/main.js` (`load`, `change`, `save`), `scripts/settings.test.ts`

### Subpixel smoothing

`trial/subpixel` (merged into `master`, fork only): subpixel (LCD, liquid crystal display) text smoothing on macOS, off by default. `text_rendering` (terminals) and `ui_text_rendering` (panels) take `"grayscale"` (default) or `"subpixel"`; `subpixel_order` takes `"rgb"` (default) or `"bgr"`. Subpixel only draws on an opaque window on a 1x, unrotated display; on the 2x displays and anywhere else text stays grayscale, and a window moved between displays switches on its next frame. It started as a trial of the experiment listed under "Rejected text experiments" below, judged on the 1x LG display: the interface uses it there; terminal text stays grayscale, which matches Ghostty.

CoreGraphics makes no per-channel coverage on macOS 27, so subpixel glyphs are rasterised by swash. CoreText still shapes, picks glyphs and lays out advances (they match swash's to 0.00000 px). swash draws the outline at 3× horizontal resolution (`Render::transform(scale(3, 1))`) and FreeType's default 5-tap filter, [8, 77, 86, 77, 8]/256, turns each pixel's three subpixels into red, green and blue coverage (`vendor/gpui-pre-macos/src/lcd.rs`). Faces are opened once per font from the bytes Herdr embedded, a mapped single-face file, or the tables CoreText hands out (collections), at the variation CoreText draws. swash has no smoothing dilation and its plain coverage is about 17% lighter than CoreGraphics' (JetBrains Mono 12), and CoreGraphics grows glyphs more along the rows than across them. So outlines are emboldened separately across and along the rows (`Outline::embolden(x, y)`), by one growth per font (face, size in device pixels, and thicken or the color's dilation): the growth that inks a calibration set exactly like CoreGraphics' grayscale while matching each glyph's rows' ink first and its pixels second (`lcd::fit`: least squares, linear in the growth, under the ink constraint). The set is printable ASCII (American Standard Code for Information Interchange) with lowercase counted twice (evenly, Geist 13 inked 2% over grayscale on interface text; lowercase alone, 1–3% under). It is fitted once, when the font's first subpixel glyph enters the atlas (0.7–3.9 ms in a debug build, each glyph drawn by swash plain and grown 0.1 px each way at one sample per pixel in CoreGraphics' box), and cached. With `font_thicken` on, CoreGraphics draws the set at smoothing levels 0 and 4 and the growth for any level is interpolated between the two fits (`lcd::Weight`); the level is a continuous function of the strength (`lcd::thicken_level`): 0 at strength 0, through the middle of each of CoreGraphics' levels 1 to 3, to 4 at 255. CoreGraphics' own levels step at strengths 100, 165, 208 and 241, so grayscale adds no weight below 100 and jumps about 5.5% at each step, where subpixel adds some at every strength and lands within three quarters of a step of grayscale everywhere. With it `"auto"`, the fit is against grayscale at the color's dilation. With it `false`, the fit is against CoreGraphics' unsmoothed grayscale (`SubpixelMatch::Unsmoothed`, one steady growth): JetBrains Mono 12 grows −0.037 px across by 0.088 along, Geist 13 0.004 by −0.019, so swash's plain outline is nearly CoreGraphics' unsmoothed one and "off" adds no smoothing dilation. Each fit shrinks along one axis, so the move that keeps a backwards-wound glyph in place after emboldening follows the glyph's outward sign (`lcd::Glyph::outward`), not the growth's sign: a normally wound glyph grown by a negative strength stays where swash puts it. The fit, not zero growth, is kept: at zero growth the terminal line inks 2.5% under grayscale, its ascender rows 6.7% under and the x-height's top row 16.9% of the peak row under; fitted, 0.8% and 0.5% under and 8.1%. That row is where CoreGraphics' unsmoothed edges sit a little higher than swash's, which one growth for the whole font can't follow, so `check_shape` allows 9% for off (`OFF_ROW_TOLERANCE`) against 5% for auto and 4% thickened. Fitted values: JetBrains Mono 12 at strength 30, 0.073 px across by 0.117 along (0.063 by 0.115 at level 0, 0.230 by 0.150 at 4); Geist 13 at strength 192, 0.198 by 0.093. A growth fitted to each glyph (an earlier build) matched each glyph's ink and rows but gave neighbouring glyphs different stems: `i` 22% wider than `l`. swash reads one winding for a whole outline and gets some glyphs' wrong (JetBrains Mono's `i`, whose two contours its whole-outline area test reads as one backwards polygon), so a positive growth shrinks them: each glyph's sign comes from whether swash's probe growth adds ink (`lcd::Samples::outward`, cached per glyph), and it grows by the font's growth times that sign. swash grows an outline right and up, its left and bottom edges fixed; grown by a negative strength it grows left and down, so those glyphs are moved back by twice the strength to sit as the rest do. A face swash can't open falls back to its grayscale coverage. Emoji stay as they are.

Metal gains what Zed's Linux and Windows backends have: a BGRA (blue, green, red, alpha) `Subpixel` atlas kind, a `subpixel_sprite` shader pair whose second output blends each background channel toward the text color by its own coverage (dual-source blending, `MTLBlendFactor::Source1Color`), and the `SubpixelSprites` batch. Blending happens in the same gamma space as monochrome sprites, with no Zed gamma correction, so three equal coverages draw exactly as grayscale. `text_contrast` applies per channel. `SubpixelSprite`'s alignment `pad` became `contrast` (same layout; the wgpu and DirectX shaders still read it as pad). `Window::with_glyph_subpixel` / `set_glyph_subpixel` ask for subpixel glyphs under the app's platform-default mode, as the thicken and contrast pairs do; `should_use_subpixel_rendering` still requires an opaque window and `is_subpixel_rendering_supported()`, which macOS answers from the window's screen (backing scale 1, `CGDisplayRotation` 0), cached and re-checked when the screen or scale changes. `gpui::set_subpixel_bgr` swaps channels in the shader.

- Measured (`tests/subpixel_text.rs`, headless Metal at 1x, painted as the app paints the terminal grid and panels). Ink is the mean coverage over the text's box; the test asserts subpixel within 2% of grayscale with thicken on, 5% with it auto or off, ink rising with strength, every printable ASCII glyph inking more at 255 than at 0, a rasteriser sweep of every strength 0 to 255 within 5%, and shape (below). Thickened text inks alike in both polarities, so one row per strength:

| Text | Thicken | Grayscale | Subpixel | Difference | Linear-light difference (light on dark / dark on light) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Terminal: JetBrains Mono 12, contrast 0.2 | 0 | 0.0940 | 0.0935 | −0.5% | −2.4% / +0.9% |
| Terminal | 30 | 0.0940 | 0.0946 | +0.7% | −0.8% / +1.8% |
| Terminal | 112 | 0.0990 | 0.0978 | −1.2% | −3.1% / +0.1% |
| Terminal | 192 | 0.1041 | 0.1043 | +0.3% | −0.8% / +1.1% |
| Terminal | 255 | 0.1139 | 0.1137 | −0.2% | −1.0% / +0.5% |
| Panel: Geist 13, contrast 0.2 | 0 | 0.1271 | 0.1272 | +0.1% | +0.9% / −0.3% |
| Panel | 30 | 0.1271 | 0.1289 | +1.5% | +2.6% / +0.9% |
| Panel | 112 | 0.1345 | 0.1336 | −0.7% | −0.2% / −0.9% |
| Panel | 192 | 0.1418 | 0.1432 | +1.0% | +1.3% / +1.1% |
| Panel | 255 | 0.1566 | 0.1574 | +0.5% | −0.8% / +1.7% |

- Thicken auto: terminal light on dark 0.1139 against 0.1137 (−0.2%), dark on light 0.0940 against 0.0935 (−0.5%); panel 0.1566 against 0.1574 (+0.5%) and 0.1271 against 0.1272 (+0.1%).
- Thicken off (smoothing off), both polarities alike: terminal 0.0818 against 0.0811 (−0.8%), panel 0.1097 against 0.1096 (−0.1%). Shape: total −0.5%, worst row −8.1% (row 7 above the baseline), ascender rows +0.5%, x-height band +0.6%; stems 1.00 px against grayscale's 1.09 (lowercase `l`). Rasterising costs 3.6 µs per subpixel glyph against grayscale's 2.3 µs (debug build).
- Shape: the person's three test lines in JetBrains Mono 12 (contrast 0.2), each glyph painted at its whole-pixel cell (7 px) on 16 px rows, as the grid paints them. Per pixel row, summed over the three lines, subpixel stays within 4% of the peak row's ink of grayscale's (5% with thicken auto, 9% off), the ascender rows and the x-height band each within 3%, the lines' total ink within 3%, and the glyphs' mean vertical spread within 0.04 rows. Strength 30: total +1.4%, worst row +2.6% (row 6 above the baseline), ascender rows +1.9%, x-height band +1.8%; strength 0: +0.2%, −3.7% (row 7), +0.7% / +0.6%; thicken auto light on dark +0.4%, −3.9% (row 2), +0.5% / −0.1%. Per-glyph growth had matched the worst rows to 1.4–4.1%; isotropic emboldening missed them by 5–7%.
- Stems: the width of the vertical stems of `l i n m h u 1 I |`, each between spaces (ink over two rows of the x-height between arches and serifs, per row and stem), and their spread across the glyphs (standard deviation over mean). Subpixel's spread may not exceed grayscale's by more than 1 point. Strength 30: grayscale 3.0%, per-font growth 2.9% (each glyph's width over grayscale's spreads 1.8%), per-glyph growth 8.8% (7.3%; `i` 1.51 px against grayscale's 1.24), isotropic per-glyph emboldening 2.8% (1.7%). Mean stem width at strength 30: grayscale 1.21 px, per-font 1.22, isotropic 1.24.
- Linear light (the column above decodes sRGB before taking ink) is not what the shader blends in, but it is closer to what the display emits: there subpixel light-on-dark text reads 0.5–3% lighter than grayscale and dark-on-light 0.3–2.3% heavier, because the filter spreads coverage over more, fainter pixels. Terminal text on the LG measured 8.5% lighter than Ghostty at strength 30 with an uncalibrated build (fixed emboldening, `i` shrunk), then 8–10% heavier with isotropic per-glyph emboldening, its ascender tops about 10% light and x-height band about 20% heavy (screenshots downscaled 3440 to 2000 px). Headless, that build's rows strayed 5–7% of the peak row at most; no headless metric reproduces the display's size of gap, so judge it on the display.
- Cost: swash draws a glyph in 2.1–2.2 µs against CoreGraphics' 2.8–3.1 µs (release). Once a font is calibrated, a subpixel glyph costs 4.2 µs with thicken 30 and 3.5 µs with it auto, against grayscale's 2.6–2.7 µs, in a debug build (the first time a glyph is seen, three one-sample-per-pixel swash draws find its sign). The first 1x frame of a full 220×50 terminal screen draws in 1.6–1.8 ms either way in a debug build. Subpixel tiles are 4.7–4.9× the atlas bytes (BGRA, 1 pixel wider each side): all printable ASCII at 12 px in its 4 subpixel positions is 176–247 KB against 36–52 KB. A window's first subpixel atlas page is 4 MiB (1024² BGRA) against 1 MiB for grayscale.
- Off, nothing changes: grayscale renders of the terminal and panel fonts were byte-identical to `master` at 1x and 2x, and at 2x a paint asking for subpixel is byte-identical to grayscale (asserted).
- Rough edges: the outlines are unhinted, as CoreGraphics' are; colored fringes show in screenshots and screen shares; a display rotated while a window stays on it isn't noticed until the window moves or resizes; there's no per-display order, so `subpixel_order` applies to every 1x display.
- Turn on: `"text_rendering": "subpixel"` and/or `"ui_text_rendering": "subpixel"` in `~/.config/herdr-app/settings.json`, or the Settings window's Text > Rendering page ("Text smoothing" under Terminal text, "Interface text smoothing" under Interface text). Turn off: remove the keys or pick Grayscale; both apply live.
- Files: `vendor/gpui-pre-macos/src/{lcd.rs,text_system.rs,window.rs,gpui_macos.rs}`, `vendor/gpui-pre-macos/Cargo.toml` (`swash`, `memmap2`), `vendor/gpui-pre-apple/{src/metal_atlas.rs,src/metal_renderer.rs,src/shaders.metal,build.rs,vendor/gpui/src/scene.rs}`, `vendor/gpui-pre/src/{scene.rs,window.rs,platform.rs,platform/test/window.rs,app/headless_app_context.rs}`, `src/settings.rs`, `src/terminal.rs`, `src/window.rs`, `defaults/AGENTS.md`, `defaults/frame/settings/main.js`, `scripts/settings.test.ts`, `tests/subpixel_text.rs`. The filter's, `thicken_level`'s, `Weight`'s and `Samples::growth`'s unit tests run with `cargo test -p gpui-pre-macos --lib lcd`.
- Upstream: fork only, trial

### Handlers inside list rows

`feat/vlist-row-handlers` (branched from `master`, fork only): a row a lazy list's item renderer returns (`v_virtual_list`, `h_virtual_list`, `uniform_list`, `list`) may register its own handlers: `on_click`, `on_hover`, `on_mouse_down`/`on_mouse_up` (any button), `on_mouse_move`, `on_action`, `on_open_change` and the rest of gpui-kit's handler methods, on the row and on anything inside it, including a `HoverCard`'s trigger and content. `cx.stop_propagation()` in a row's handler keeps the press from the list's `on_item_click`, which works too. Upstream refuses all of these with an error naming `on_item_click`, because a handler belongs to the snapshot that registered it and a row is rebuilt every frame, so per-row handlers would pile up for as long as the view stood.

Each batch the item renderer describes records its handlers into a callback generation of its own (`render_virtual_items`) and commits it with a `CallbackLease` that retires the generation on drop. Every row of the batch holds the lease from a no-op mouse-move listener (`holding`), which GPUI keeps from a painted element until the next frame's listeners replace it, so a batch lives exactly as long as its rows are on screen. The measuring pass's batch is never painted and is released when layout drops it, and a batch that registered nothing takes no lease and no wrapper. A handler belongs to the row it was drawn into, so a press after a scroll reaches the row that is under the pointer. Layout-phase renderers other than a list's item renderer (a dock's chrome, a registered component's element callback) still refuse handlers, with a message that doesn't point at `on_item_click`.

- Row state a handler changes (hovered, pressed, dragging) lives on the view, keyed by the list's `get_key`: a row remembers nothing between frames. A `HoverCard` in a row keeps its open state in GPUI's window state under its id, so its id must be unique per row (`card-${key}`).
- Files: `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/engine/quickjs/mod.rs` (`InteractiveLayoutGuard`, `render_virtual_items`, `layout_handler_refusal`), `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/snapshot.rs` (`CallbackLease`), `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/spec.rs` (`ItemSpecs::lease`), `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/materialize/components/virtual_list.rs` (`render_range`, `holding`), `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/typings.rs` (`on_item_click`, `v_virtual_list`), `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/{materialize.rs,engine/mod.rs}` (docs), `vendor/gpui-kit/crates/shell/src/tests/render.rs` (tests `a_row_registers_handlers_of_its_own`, `a_rows_handler_fires_for_the_row_under_the_pointer_after_a_scroll`, `a_rows_hover_and_mouse_handlers_see_the_row_they_are_on`, `a_rows_own_handlers_do_not_accumulate_while_it_is_scrolled`, `a_hover_card_inside_a_row_opens_and_its_content_handles_a_click`), `src/mods/lifecycle_tests.rs` (test `virtual_list_rows_carry_their_own_handlers`)
- Upstream: none yet

### Dim when inactive

`feat/unfocused-dim` and `feat/window-dim` (branched from `master`, fork only): `unfocused_dim` in settings.json (0 to 1, default 0, off), as Rex does, with `unfocused_dim_scope` choosing what fades while the Herdr window isn't the active one. The switch is instant both ways, with no fade, and costs one redraw per activation change and nothing while it holds.

- `"window"` (the default): the whole window fades `unfocused_dim` toward `unfocused_dim_color` (`"#rrggbb"`; unset, the theme's `background`). A mid grey lightens backgrounds while it darkens text, as Rex's inactive window does (measured on Rex: about luma 77 at 25%). A value that isn't `"#rrggbb"` is a `problems()` entry and the background is used (`Settings::dim_color`). A `HERDR:` change in `vendor/gpui-pre/src/window.rs` gives the window a veil (`Window::set_veil`): one full-window quad of that color, painted at the end of `draw_roots`, after the root, the deferred draws (whatever their priority), prompts, drags and tooltips. The scene orders a primitive above everything it overlaps, so a quad covering the window is the topmost primitive of the frame. A quad has no hitbox, so the mouse goes through to what is under it. `AppWindow::render` sets the veil from `inactive_veil`, beside `set_glyph_thicken`; GPUI refreshes the whole window when activation changes, so render reruns at the moment of the change. The terminal text fade stays off, so nothing is dimmed twice and the row cache keeps its rows.
- `"text"`: each terminal's text, sprites, fallback icons and link underlines blend that fraction toward the terminal's background. Backgrounds, selection and find highlights (painted from the frame, outside the text's colours), the cursor and the panels keep their colours. `paint_grid` reads GPUI's `window.is_window_active()` rather than the window's polled `window_active`, which would lag by up to 400 ms. The dim is applied while a row's drawing is built (`row_ops`, through `Fade`), since shaped lines carry their colours. The effective dim and the background it fades toward are part of `painted_with`, so a change of either rebuilds every row once and a steady state reuses the kept rows.
- Settings window: on the Appearance > Window page under Inactive window, a "Dim when inactive" stepper (steps of 0.1, through the same `contrast` helper as `text_contrast`) and a "Dim" picker (Window or Text). The setting sits on Window rather than Rendering because its default reaches the whole window. Layouts don't set either.
- Files: `src/settings.rs` (`DimScope`, `Settings::dim_color`, tests `unfocused_dim_defaults_off_and_validates`, `unfocused_dim_scope_defaults_to_the_window_and_refuses_others`, `unfocused_dim_color_is_a_hex_color_or_the_background`), `src/terminal.rs` (`dimmed`, `Fade`, `paint_grid`, test `dimming_blends_text_toward_the_background`), `src/window.rs` (`inactive_veil`, `AppWindow::render`, tests `terminal_text_dims_while_the_window_is_inactive`, `the_whole_window_dims_while_inactive`, `the_veil_covers_deferred_draws`), `vendor/gpui-pre/src/window.rs` (`set_veil`, `draw_roots`, `painted_orders`), `defaults/AGENTS.md`, `defaults/frame/settings/main.js`, `scripts/settings.test.ts`
- Upstream: none yet

### Session resume

`feat/session-resume` (branched from `master`, fork only): agents come back after a reboot. herdr-core brings kept terminals back at their addresses as fresh shells in their last folder; Herdr records, while they run, the command line that resumes each program with a `resume` rule in the programs table, and types it back into terminals that are bare shells again. It replaces the `herdr-resume` save/restore script with something always current.

- Rule: `resume` on a `programs.json` entry: `session` (`file` with `{pid}` and `~/`, `field`, `when` equalities), `drop` (flags with how many values each takes at most; a value never starts with `-`), `append` (`{session_id}`), or `{ "rerun": true }`; `{}` turns a shipped rule off. Rules are checked when the table loads: an `append` word or a session id may only use letters, digits and `_ . / : = + -`, and an id is at most 256 bytes and never starts with `-`, so an id is never a flag or shell syntax; other kept words are shell-quoted. Bad rules are dropped and named in `programs.error`. Only Claude Code ships one (`~/.claude/sessions/<pid>.json`, `kind: interactive`); the other agents have resume flags but no file mapping a process to its session, so they need the agent to report it.
- Session source: the terminal label `herdr.session.<program key>=<id>` (or `<id>@<pid>`, honored only for that process, so a nested `claude -p` can't overwrite its parent's report; empty clears it) wins over the session file. The label lives in herdr-core, so a hook can report while the app is closed and from other machines. The file is read where the program runs: on this machine directly, on another through the core's `file.read` with `on`. Claude Code writes its file once at start, so after `/clear` or `/resume` inside it only the label is current.
- Records: `data/resume.json` in the app home, keyed by terminal, each with `terminal`, `where`, `program`, `name`, `cwd`, the resuming `argv`, `session`, `saved_ms`. A lookup runs when a resumable program's process id, turn or reported session changes (`Tracker::observe`, off the UI thread); a record is rewritten only when its content changes. It is removed when the app sees the program end in a terminal whose own process stayed the same (the person quit it), sees the terminal go, or a lookup replaces it. A reboot, `hc server stop`/start or a lost-and-restarted terminal (the terminal's process changes, and a reconnect forgets what was seen) keep it.
- Offer: for 60 s after the app starts or reconnects to the core, each listing checks for records whose terminal is live with a bare shell (no foreground, or a shell from the table) and a session no other record shares. The first time there are any, `resume_agents` decides: `ask` (default) shows one toast "Resume N sessions" whose click runs `session.resume {confirmed: true}`; `auto` resumes them at once; `off` does nothing. Settings > Agents > Activity has it under After a restart, as "Resume agents after restart" (Ask / Auto / Off).
- Commands: `session.resume {terminal?, confirmed?}` sends `cd <cwd> && <argv>` (PowerShell: `Set-Location -LiteralPath …; & …`) through the core's `terminal.send` to each ready terminal, skipping the rest with why; the person is asked first (a confirm dialog) unless confirmed, scripts must pass `confirmed: true`. `session.saved` lists the records with `ready` or `why`. The palette has "Resume sessions". `herdr-app do session.resume --confirmed true` and `herdr-app do session.saved` are the script paths.

### Faint opacity

`feat/faint-and-fallbacks` (branched from `master`, fork only): `faint_opacity` in settings.json (0 to 1, default 0.5) is the opacity faint (SGR 2, Select Graphic Rendition) terminal text draws at, Ghostty's `faint-opacity` with its default. Upstream draws faint text at a fixed 0.6, which measured brighter than Ghostty beside it (brightest dim pixel 125 against Ghostty's 108, fg 195 on bg 33). `Settings::faint_alpha` clamps it; a value outside 0..1 is a `problems()` entry. `row_ops` sets faint cells' alpha from it, and `painted_with` carries it so a change redraws kept rows (test `kept_rows_rebuild_when_faint_opacity_or_font_fallbacks_change`).

- Settings window: a "Faint text" stepper (steps of 0.1, through the `contrast` helper, which takes the default to leave out of settings.json) under "Contrast" in the Text > Rendering page's "Terminal text" group. Layouts don't set it.
